It’s more that I get concerned when I can’t stay on track, but I wouldn’t say I’m just worrying all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven’t noticed feeling sad or down lately. It’s more frustration when I’m struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not like I forget who people are or anything, but I forget things I meant to do—routine things. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still somehow miss it or forget why I set it. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any emotional distress or feelings of overwhelm when you're unable to stay on track?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I can’t stay on track, I do feel overwhelmed, especially at work. It’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That feels awful—like I’m becoming a liability instead of the person who’s supposed to be leading.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That’s the main place it’s causing problems—on the floor, little things like an IV pump beeping or a call light going off will completely pull me away from what I was doing, and then I’ve lost my train of thought. I don’t think I’m worrying more than I used to, at least not in a way I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I get concerned when I can’t stay on track, but I wouldn’t say I’m just worrying all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven’t noticed feeling sad or down lately. It’s more frustration when I’m struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not like I forget who people are or anything, but I forget things I meant to do—routine things. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any emotional distress or feelings of overwhelm when you're unable to stay on track?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I can’t stay on track, I do feel overwhelmed, especially at work. It’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That feels awful—like I’m becoming a liability instead of the person who’s supposed to be leading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any emotional distress or feelings of overwhelm when you're unable to stay on track?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I can’t stay on track, I do feel overwhelmed, especially at work. It’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That feels awful—like I’m becoming a liability instead of the person who’s supposed to be leading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I do get tense or worried sometimes when I’m behind or things are piling up, but I haven’t noticed constant anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down and do them. I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. The big one is anything that takes a lot of focused chart review. Like if I need to audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep putting it off and find bedside tasks to do instead. Sitting down with it just feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things that aren't necessarily important or urgent?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s honestly one of the main reasons I came in. At work on the floor, I lose my train of thought all the time. Like an IV pump will beep, or someone will ask another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I can’t remember what patient flow issue I was in the middle of dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical complaints, such as headaches or fatigue?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired sometimes like anyone working shifts, but I haven’t noticed headaches or any physical complaints that feel unusual or like a main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these problems with focus and memory at any time other than when you're at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. At home I’ll forget things that should be routine, like paying a bill or calling a family member back. And sometimes I’ll mean to follow up on something, even set a reminder, and then still somehow miss it. I’ve also forgotten appointments, which is frustrating because I really did intend to go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if the patient's symptoms are significantly impacting daily life outside of work", "question": "Are you having difficulty completing daily tasks or responsibilities at home due to your trouble with focus and memory?"}

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time staying focused at work. I’m a nurse, and on the floor I keep losing my train of thought—like an IV pump will beep, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I can’t remember what patient flow issue I was just working on. It’s happening constantly, and it’s really starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing or staying on task outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I notice it most at work, honestly. That’s the main place it’s causing problems—on the floor, little things like an IV pump beeping or a call light going off will completely pull me away from what I was doing, and then I’ve lost my train of thought. Outside of work, I’m not sure it stands out the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m worrying more than I used to, at least not in a way I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I get concerned when I can’t stay on track, but I wouldn’t say I’m just worrying all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven’t noticed feeling sad or down lately. It’s more frustration when I’m struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not like I forget who people are or anything, but I forget things I meant to do—routine things. Like calling a family member back, following up on a missing order, paying a bill, or even showing up for an appointment. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still somehow miss it or forget why I set it. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any emotional distress or feelings of overwhelm when you're unable to stay on track?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I can’t stay on track, I do feel overwhelmed, especially at work. It’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That feels awful—like I’m becoming a liability instead of the person who’s supposed to be leading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I do get tense or worried sometimes when I’m behind or things are piling up, but I haven’t noticed constant anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down and do them. I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. The big one is anything that takes a lot of focused chart review. Like if I need to audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep putting it off and find bedside tasks to do instead. Sitting down with it just feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things that aren't necessarily important or urgent?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s honestly one of the main reasons I came in. At work on the floor, I lose my train of thought all the time. Like an IV pump will beep, or someone will ask another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I can’t remember what patient flow issue I was in the middle of dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical complaints, such as headaches or fatigue?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired sometimes like anyone working shifts, but I haven’t noticed headaches or any physical complaints that feel unusual or like a main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these problems with focus and memory at any time other than when you're at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. At home I’ll forget things that should be routine, like paying a bill or calling a family member back. And sometimes I’ll mean to follow up on something, even set a reminder, and then still somehow miss it. I’ve also forgotten appointments, which is frustrating because I really did intend to go.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing daily tasks or responsibilities at home due to your trouble with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes. At home it gets in the way more than I’d like. I’ll forget routine things, like paying a bill or calling a family member back, even when I meant to do it. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still miss the follow-up somehow, or I’ll forget an appointment. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care—I just lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: It’s been a major problem for almost two years now, since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities. I had little issues before, but that’s when it really started affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’ll forget to call a family member back even when I really meant to, and then it seems like I’m ignoring them. I’ve also missed appointments or forgotten to follow up on things, even after setting reminders. It makes me feel unreliable, and I know it can be frustrating for the people close to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I mean, I get tired sometimes like anyone working shifts, but I haven’t noticed headaches or any physical complaints that feel unusual or like a main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these problems with focus and memory at any time other than when you're at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. At home I’ll forget things that should be routine, like paying a bill or calling a family member back. And sometimes I’ll mean to follow up on something, even set a reminder, and then still somehow miss it. I’ve also forgotten appointments, which is frustrating because I really did intend to go.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing daily tasks or responsibilities at home due to your trouble with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes. At home it gets in the way more than I’d like. I’ll forget routine things, like paying a bill or calling a family member back, even when I meant to do it. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still miss the follow-up somehow, or I’ll forget an appointment. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care—I just lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: It’s been a major problem for almost two years now, since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities. I had little issues before, but that’s when it really started affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’ll forget to call a family member back even when I really meant to, and then it seems like I’m ignoring them. I’ve also missed appointments or forgotten to follow up on things, even after setting reminders. It makes me feel unreliable, and I know it can be frustrating for the people close to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make any significant changes to your daily routine or responsibilities because of your trouble with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest change has been at work, honestly. Things got serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That’s been hard to accept. I’m supposed to be leading, but it feels like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and memory have changed how others perceive you or your role?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work especially, I feel like people don’t see me as the dependable charge nurse the same way anymore. Being on formal supervision and having other nurses double-check my follow-through makes me feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time staying focused at work. I’m a nurse, and on the floor I keep losing my train of thought—like an IV pump will beep, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I can’t remember what patient flow issue I was just working on. It’s happening constantly, and it’s really starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing or staying on task outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I notice it most at work, honestly. That’s the main place it’s causing problems—on the floor, little things like an IV pump beeping or a call light going off will completely pull me away from what I was doing, and then I’ve lost my train of thought. Outside of work, I’m not sure it stands out the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m worrying more than I used to, at least not in a way I’ve really noticed. It’s more that I get concerned when I can’t stay on track, but I wouldn’t say I’m just worrying all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven’t noticed feeling sad or down lately. It’s more frustration when I’m struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not like I forget who people are or anything, but I forget things I meant to do—routine things. Like calling a family member back, following up on a missing order, paying a bill, or even showing up for an appointment. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still somehow miss it or forget why I set it. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any emotional distress or feelings of overwhelm when you're unable to stay on track?\n\nPatient: Yes. When I can’t stay on track, I do feel overwhelmed, especially at work. It’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That feels awful—like I’m becoming a liability instead of the person who’s supposed to be leading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I do get tense or worried sometimes when I’m behind or things are piling up, but I haven’t noticed constant anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I still enjoy the things I usually like when I actually sit down and do them. I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest, no.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations that you've been avoiding lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. The big one is anything that takes a lot of focused chart review. Like if I need to audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep putting it off and find bedside tasks to do instead. Sitting down with it just feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things that aren't necessarily important or urgent?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s honestly one of the main reasons I came in. At work on the floor, I lose my train of thought all the time. Like an IV pump will beep, or someone will ask another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I can’t remember what patient flow issue I was in the middle of dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical complaints, such as headaches or fatigue?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired sometimes like anyone working shifts, but I haven’t noticed headaches or any physical complaints that feel unusual or like a main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these problems with focus and memory at any time other than when you're at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. At home I’ll forget things that should be routine, like paying a bill or calling a family member back. And sometimes I’ll mean to follow up on something, even set a reminder, and then still somehow miss it. I’ve also forgotten appointments, which is frustrating because I really did intend to go.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty completing daily tasks or responsibilities at home due to your trouble with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes. At home it gets in the way more than I’d like. I’ll forget routine things, like paying a bill or calling a family member back, even when I meant to do it. Sometimes I’ll set a reminder and still miss the follow-up somehow, or I’ll forget an appointment. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care—I just lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: It’s been a major problem for almost two years now, since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities. I had little issues before, but that’s when it really started affecting me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and memory affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’ll forget to call a family member back even when I really meant to, and then it seems like I’m ignoring them. I’ve also missed appointments or forgotten to follow up on things, even after setting reminders. It makes me feel unreliable, and I know it can be frustrating for the people close to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make any significant changes to your daily routine or responsibilities because of your trouble with focus and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest change has been at work, honestly. Things got serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That’s been hard to accept. I’m supposed to be leading, but it feels like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and memory have changed how others perceive you or your role?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work especially, I feel like people don’t see me as the dependable charge nurse the same way anymore. Being on formal supervision and having other nurses double-check my follow-through makes me feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and memory have affected your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s affected my quality of life a lot, mostly through work. The concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. That’s stressful and honestly embarrassing. I feel like I’m supposed to be leading, but instead I’m starting to feel like a liability.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or illnesses that could have affected your brain function?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I haven’t had any head injury or illness that I know of that would explain it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
